### Page 2

2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
⚫ 歐洲央行上周四(9/10)為年內第二次上調利率，以應對能源價格上漲帶來的通膨
壓力 ， 並警告物價壓力可能比先前預期更久 ， 推動市場加大對後續進一步收緊政
策的押注 。 歐洲央行總裁拉加德形容此次决定爲”無需多想的决定”， 並警告通膨
回落至 2%目標的時間點目前預計在 2027 年底。歐洲央行預期明年平均通膨爲
2.5%，高於 6 月的 2.3%；2027 年核心通膨預期爲 2.6%，高於此前的 2.5%。
⚫ 軍事消息人士稱，與伊朗結盟的胡塞武裝上周四(9/10)奪取葉門港口城市的控制
權 ， 並沿紅海海岸向南推進至戰略島嶼地區 。 美國總統川普表示 ， 他預計伊朗戰
爭將在美國中期選舉後結束。若胡塞武裝控制這條與 荷姆茲海峽同樣重要的水
道 ， 其支持者伊朗將在與美國的戰爭中獲得關鍵戰略優勢 ， 並進一步推高油價 。
⚫ 美國勞工部勞工統計局上週四(9/10)公布 8 月生産者物價指數 PPI 年增 5.4%，

高於市場預期的 5.3％。週五(9/11)公布 8 月消費者物價指數(CPI)年增 3.4%與前
月相同，核心 CPI 年增率 2.4%符合市場預期，月增率達 0.3%高於市場預期。
FED WATCH顯示數據公布後推升市場對於聯準會本周將升息的預期。
⚫ 日本央行數據顯示，8 月國內企業物價指數較去年同期上漲 7.6%。增幅高於市
場預估中值 7.4%。7 月漲幅經修正後爲 7.7%；8 月進口物價指數同比上漲 24.8%，
7 月修正後爲上漲 29.3%， 表明當月日圓貶值以及中東局勢影響推高進口成本和
整體通膨，同時鞏固市場對日本央行在 9 月會議進一步升息預期。

### Page 3

3  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
美元兌新台幣匯率走勢
Sep/14/2026
＜上週走勢＞
上週台幣匯價高點為 31.455，低點在 31.698，以 31.638 作收，較前一週收盤價
貶值 0.8 分。
上週台幣先升後貶，週初，受到市場對於日本央行 9 月升息的預期升溫，日圓
走升帶動美元指數跌破 99 整數價位低檔震盪 ， 加以台股高檔震盪 ， 外資延續匯入的
走勢，帶動台幣升破31.500 至 31.455 週內高點；臨近週末，美國公布8 月生產者物
價指數年增率由上個月的 4.8%上升至 5.4%，數據公布後，FEDWATCH顯示聯準會
9 月升息機率攀升至 7 成，美元指數回到 99 上方，台股高點回落，外資偏向匯出，
台幣由升轉貶至 31.698 週內低點，終場在央行調節之下收在 31.638。
＜未來分析＞
預估本週台幣區間震盪。國際市場方面，上週五公布美國 8 月核心消費者物價
指數月增率 0.3%高於市場預期的 0.2%，數據公布後，提高本週聯準會升息的機率

至接近 9 成，此外中東局勢仍有升溫風險，油價持續高檔震盪，美元指數在 99 整數
關口盤整；就台灣市場來看，台灣 8 月出口衝上 824 億美元，創下歷年單月新高紀
錄，月增 9.4%，年增 41%，展現極為強勁的成長動能。顯示受惠於 AI 需求全面爆
發，帶動外銷出口暢旺，基本面強勁持續支撐台幣的表現；盤勢上，匯價逢低吸引
進口商及政府基金買盤進場，逢高則面臨出口商賣美金，且本週台美均有利率決策
會議，預估匯率波動將加劇。綜合以上，預估本週台幣走勢區間震盪。
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌台幣：31.400-31.900

### Page 4

4  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌人民幣匯率走勢  
Sep/14/2026 
＜上週走勢＞ 
    上週人民幣震盪走升。週初，雖然美伊戰火未平，帶動油價及美債利率高漲，
但市場對於日本央行 9 月升息預期升溫，日圓偏升帶動美元指數跌破 99 價位後低
檔震盪，人民幣延續偏升走勢，走升至6.7022 週內高點；臨近週末，美國公布8 月
生產者物價指數 5.4%優於市場預期 5.3%， 加以8 月核心消費者物價指數月增率 0.3%
優於市場預期的 0.2%，數據公布後，FEDWATCH顯示本週聯準會 9 月升息預期大
幅攀升至近 9 成，美元指數回到99 上方，人民幣回吐部分週初以來的漲幅，終場境
外人民幣收在 6.7071，週線升值 8 點，境內人民幣則收在6.7079，週線升值 29 點。  
＜未來分析＞ 
     預估本週人民幣區間偏升。國際市場方面，美國通膨數據表現優於市場預期，
加以油價攀升至每桶 100 美元上方 ， 美債殖利率位居高檔 ， 美元指數也在99 上方盤
整，關注本週聯準會最新利率決議，預計在會議結果出爐以前，聯準會升息預期將
支撐美元指數，限制人民幣升值的空間。中國國內方面，人行中間價格緩步偏升，
上週升破 6.7800 價位，持續釋放人民幣偏強信號，支撐人民幣延續升值的方向。技
術面方面 ，USD/CNH 匯價居於各天期均線下方 ，且均線向下發散 ，加以匯價由低點
向上反彈測試 6.7150 關鍵價位後面臨壓力，顯示空方動能較盛，預計人民幣將延續
升值的態勢。綜合以上，預估本週人民幣走勢區間偏升。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌人民幣：6.6800-6.7300

### Page 5

5  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌日圓匯率走勢  
         Sep/14/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
    上週 USD/JPY 震盪下跌 。週初 ，因市場預期日本央行將加快升息步伐及資金將
回流日本，使 USD/JPY 跌破至 155 整數關卡之下；週間，USD/JPY 延續跌勢最低
來到 152.87 後反彈，後續大致在 153 至 154 間波動；臨近週末，美國公布 8 月 PPI
及 CPI 數據顯示通膨仍處高位 ，USD/JPY 一度反彈至154.6 後回落 ， 終場收在153.54，
週線大跌 270 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 USD/JPY 區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、上週公布日本第二季 GDP
較上季增長 0.4%，季增年率也由 1.1%上升至 1.4%，主因為企業資本支出改善，而
7 月名目薪資年增 4.7%為 30 年來最大漲幅，而實質薪資年增 2.4%也為 5 年多來最
大漲幅，顯示日本經濟復甦力道較原先預期強勁，進一步強化市場對於日央升息的
預期，有利日圓表現。二、美國 8 月 CPI 及 PPI 數據顯示美國通膨仍處高位，強化
市場對聯準會升息預期，且中東局勢升溫，帶動油價突破 100 美元，預期將支撐美
元，限縮 USD/JPY 下行空間。三、就技術面而言，日線圖中，USD/JPY 匯價多次向
下測試 153 整數關卡未能有效突破，顯示下方有買盤支撐，而 155 關卡附近則為前
波壓力位，因此短線先以區間看待。綜合以上，預估USD/JPY 本週呈現區間震盪的
可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌日幣：152.00-157.00 
前週交易區間 
152.87 156.27

6  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌歐元匯率走勢  
         Sep/14/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
    上週 EUR/USD 小幅下跌。 週初 ，EUR/USD 大致在 1.1600 至 1.1635 窄幅波動；
週間，市場等待歐洲央行利率決議及 美國通膨數據，EUR/USD 持續在 1.1615 至
1.1653 波動；週四晚間公布歐洲央行利率決議如預期升息 1 碼，而美國 8 月 PPI 高
於預期使市場對聯準會本月升息預期上升，EUR/USD 下行跌破 1.16 整數關口；臨
近週末，美國公布 8 月 CPI 顯示通膨仍具韌性，帶動 EUR/USD 走低來至 1.1568，
終場收在 1.1598，週線下跌 15 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 EUR/USD 區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、上週歐洲央行公布利率決
議如預期升息一碼，總裁拉加德表示即使面對能源價格衝擊，歐洲經濟第二季依然
展現韌性，預期第三季仍會延續，但未來通膨仍面臨上行風險，偏鷹派言論使市場
對於歐洲央行未來持續升息的預期上升，將帶給歐元支撐。二、美國 8 月 PPI 月增
0.4%符合市場預期 ， 年增5.4%高於預期 ，而 8 月 CPI 月增 0.4%年增 3.4%符合預期 ，
顯示通膨仍維持高位，使市場對聯準會升息預期增加，將支撐美元，不利歐元匯價
上行。三、技術面而言，在日線圖中，EUR/USD 匯價雖一度突破 1.1630 年線但未
能站穩，顯示上方仍有賣壓，而下方1.1550 附近則有支撐，在有效突破前以區間震
盪看待。綜合以上，預估 EUR/USD 本週呈現區間震盪的可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 歐元兌美元：1.1530-1.1680 
前週交易區間 
1.1568 1.1653

7  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
                         新台幣利率走勢分析

115.09.07-115.09.11 
銀行隔夜拆款利率 0.805% ~ 0.830%  
30 天期短票利率   1.66% ~ 1.69% 
公債－10 年期-A15106 1.92 % ~ 1.921% 
－ 5 年期- A15108 1.822% ~ 1.823%

一、 台幣貨幣市場部分 ，本週央行連日減發定存單 ， 使市場資金呈現平穩態勢 ， 且
資金需求不若先前，銀行間交投較為清淡，令短率持穩於近期區間。 
週 五 隔 夜 拆 款 利 率 區 間 在 0.805%~0.830% ， 30 天 期 短 票 主 要 成 交 在
1.67%~1.69%，利率較上週上升 1 個基本點。 
二、 本週 5 年期指標券 A15108 成交在約 1.82%，10 年期指標券 A15106 成交在約
1.92%。 
美伊衝突升溫，帶動國際油價持續走揚，加劇市場對通膨之疑慮，加上美國8
月非農就業人數大增、8 月 PPI 年增率高於市場預期，市場預估聯準會下週升
息機率升至七成，帶動美債利率大幅反彈；台灣央行將於下週召開理監事會
議 ， 在台灣經濟成長與通膨維持高位下 ， 台灣央行面臨緊縮壓力 ， 預期台債殖
利率面臨走升壓力 ， 預估5 年券區間為 1.83%~1.90%，10 年券區間為 1.93%~2%。

### Page 1

評等分級：買進（預期未來六個月股價漲幅超過+30%）; 逢低買進（預期未來六個月股價漲幅介於+15%與+30%之間）; 區間操
作（預期未來六個月股價漲跌幅介於-15%與+15%之間）; 逢高賣出（預期未來六個月股價跌幅介於-15%與-30%之間）；賣出（預
期未來六個月股價跌幅超過-30%）。 
本刊所刊載之內容僅做為參考，惟已力求正確與完整，但因時間及市場客觀因素改變所造成產業、市場或個股之相關條件改變，投資人需自行考量
投資之需求與風險，本公司恕不負擔任何法律責任。任何轉載或引用本報告內容必須經本公司同意。

- 1 - 
個股報告

台達電(2308) 
缺料與物料問題，創造長線布局機會 
投資評等 目標價 
逢低買進 $2,000 
前次投資建議：2026.07.30 
「買進」，目標價: $2,000

報告日期：2026.09.16 研 究 員：邱岳霖

摘 要 及 調 整 原 因 
➢ 缺料影響營運不如預期，下修 Q3獲利預估，EPS 9元 
➢ HVDC滲透率提升，且持續海內外擴產，2027 年營收挑戰超越 1兆元，EPS 62.3元。考量
電源產業將進入全新格局且台達作為主要先行者將最為受惠，兆豐投顧維持目標價 2000 元
(約 32X 27(F)PER)，潛在上漲空間 19.8%，建議逢低買進。

基本資料 結論與建議 
2026/9/15 收盤價： 1670.00 ➢ 台達電 (2308)公告 8月營收：營收 645.9億元， MoM 
-3.7%/YoY +34.9%，Q3達成率62.4%，低於研究部預
期與市場共識，係因 MOSFET全產品線呈現缺料所
致，故研究部下修台達電 26Q3營收1960.8億元(原估
2,107.4億元 )，QoQ +7.0%/YoY +30.4% ，而先前
18.3KW PSU因電容問題有進行產品召回，不過預期本
月將處理完畢且對財務無任何影響；毛利率方面，受惠
產品組合轉佳與規模經濟抵銷原料 /電子元件漲價影
響，預估毛利率 35.8%，仍可維持與上季相當，季增
0.2ppt，反映營收下修，同步下修稅後淨利232.6億元，
QoQ -7.5%/YoY +25.0%，EPS 9元(原估10.5元) ，
2026年稅後淨利960.8億元，YoY +59.8%，EPS 37.0
元（原估40元） 。 
➢ 展望2027年，受惠CSP業者持續加大投資力道，台達
於國內外同步擴產，研究部認為HVDC較大規模量產貢
獻有望於2027年起顯現，同時液冷、電源機櫃、DC-DC
模組等新產品線持續放量，此外公司亦持續推進 SST
與SOFC等能源基礎設施長期布局，電源產業將迎來新

- 2 - 
個股報告 
資料來源：CMoney；兆豐投顧預估

營 運 分 析

台達電為全球電源管理及散熱解決方案領導廠商 
台達電(2308)成立於 1971 年並於 1988 年掛牌上市，成立初期分別投入生產
交換式電源供應器與無碳刷式直流風扇，目 前已取得全球電源產品龍頭地
位。2017 年公司啟動組織調整，以「電源及零組件」、「自動化」、「基礎
設施」、「交通」四大業務範疇，並從傳統關鍵零組件製造商逐步轉型為節
能解決方案提供者。近年隨AI 資料中心建置需求爆發，公司積極將既有電源
技術延伸至 AI伺服器電源機櫃、液冷 side car、BBU、DC-DC/AC-DC模組
及 CDU 等新產品線，目前終端應用已從傳統 NB/工業電源、資料中心跨足
至高壓供電(HVDC)與液冷散熱系統，客戶涵蓋NVIDIA與北美 CSP及 ASIC
供應鏈。

26Q2 營收比重:(1)電源及零組件 53%,包含電源、被動元件等零組件、風扇
散熱;(2)交通 5%,電動車解決方案;(3)自動化 8%,包含工業自動化,樓宇自動
化;(4)基礎設施 34%,包含資訊通訊技術(通信電源、網通交換器、資料中心
解決方案、液冷side car)、能源基礎設施(電動車充電樁、可再生能源及儲能
設備等)。受惠 AI 資料中心電源需求與規格持續提升，台達持續擴充台灣、
中國大陸、泰國與美國產能，並同步推進 800V HVDC 架構開發，全面迎接
NVIDIA 新世代 GPU 機櫃功耗大幅提升所帶動的電源與散熱升級需求。

- 3 - 
個股報告

缺料影響營運不如預期，下修 Q3獲利預估，EPS 9 元 
台達電(2308)公告 8月營收：營收 645.9 億元，MoM -3.7%/YoY +34.9%，
Q3達成率 62.4%，低於研究部預期與市場共識，係因 MOSFET 全產品線呈
現缺料所致，故研究部下修台達電 26Q3 營收 1960.8 億元(原估 2,107.4 億
元)，QoQ +7.0%/YoY +30.4%，而先前 18.3KW PSU 因電容問題有進行產
品召回，不過預期本月將處理完畢且對財務無任何影響；毛利率方面，受惠
產品組合轉佳與規模經濟抵銷原料/電子元件漲價影響，預估毛利率 35.8%，
仍可維持與上季相當，季增 0.2ppt，反映營收下修，同步下修稅後淨利 232.6
億元，QoQ -7.5%/YoY +25.0%，EPS 9元(原估 10.5 元)。

AI 電源與散熱產品持續放量，輔以新產品推出，2026 EPS 37元 
展望 2026 年，主要成長動能仍來自 AI 資料中心電源、散熱與基礎設施，在
電源方面隨著 GB300 持續放量與 H2 VR200之 18.3KW 產品開始出貨，預
期價量齊揚挹注營運動能，此外 HVDC(±400V/800V)產品於 3Q26 開始量
產，惟在初期階段預期僅小幅貢獻；在散熱產品方面(涵蓋水冷板、Manifold、
CDU及 Sidecar)在 2026 下半年可望延續成長動能，主要受惠液冷滲透率持
續提升與內容價值同步墊高。綜上所述，反映 Q3下修，同步下修台達電 2026
年營運預估：2026 年營收 7,465.4 億元，YoY +34.5%，毛利率 36.2%，年
增 1.9ppt，稅後淨利 960.8 億元，YoY +59.8%，EPS 37.0元（原估 40 元）。

HVDC滲透率提升，且持續海內外擴產，2027年營收挑戰超越 1兆元，EPS 
62.3元 
展望 2027 年，受惠 CSP業者 CAPEX 持續增長，公司為滿足訂單需求，持
續加大投資力道，2026 年資本支出約 700 億元(YoY +50%)，資金來源除自
有資金外，9/15 公告發行泰達電可交換公司債籌措 15 億美元以滿足擴產資
金需求，2027 年也可望維持高檔水準，預計於泰國、中國、台灣與美國同步
擴產，研究部認為 HVDC(±400V/800V)較大規模量產貢獻有望於 2027 年起
顯現，同時液冷、電源機櫃、DC-DC模組等新產品線持續放量，此外公司亦
持續推進 SST 與 SOFC 等能源基礎設施長期布局，電源產業將迎來新格局
與長期成長動能。兆豐投顧預估台達電 2027 年營收 1.1 兆元，YoY +43.0%，
毛利率 37%，年增 0.8ppt，稅後淨利 1617.6 億元，YoY +68.4%，EPS 62.3
元。

Document 179: AI, Ageing and the Budget_ Five Takeaways from the 2026 IGR (2026-09-30)

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI, Ageing and the Budget_ Five Takeaways from the 2026 IGR (2026-09-30).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-09-30
Pages: 15
Status: ok

### Page 1

#businessconditions #confidence
AI, Ageing and the Budget: Five Takeaways from the 2026 IGR
By Pat Bustamante
Senior Economist, Westpac
18:21 September 21 2026 Share
The 2026 Intergenerational Report is the 7th in a series of such reports, dating back to
2002. The IGR sets out a budget scenario highlighting potential budget challenges over
coming decades.

### Page 2

The familiar theme is that Australia’s ageing population will put upward pressure on expenditures and
downward pressure on revenues, creating a budget challenge.
In the 2026 edition, several forces are identified as continuing to shape Australia’s economy over the
coming decades: the artificial intelligence revolution; geopolitical fragmentation; energy transition;
ageing and the care economy; and Australia’s industrial transformation.
Below are the five key themes that stood out in this year's IGR: AI to be a game-changer for

productivity; Australians will live longer; NDIS reforms are crucial to budget sustainability, but knock on
to state budgets; Bracket creep has its limits, and future spending will need to be funded elsewhere;
and Higher costs of capital here to stay.
1. AI to be a game-changer for productivity
The IGR identifies AI as having the potential to be a game-changer for productivity growth. We agree
and have written extensively about the data centre and renewable energy investment surge. This

investment will boost value added and jobs in the near term. However, the real payoff will come when
the technology is widely adopted and businesses redesign processes to get the most out of AI.
The ultimate gains are difficult to estimate, in part because of the speed of development in the
technology. Another reason is that technological change at this scale does not simply augment or
automate existing roles but creates new roles, services and goods. The Productivity Commission

estimates that AI could boost multifactor productivity in Australia by at least 2.3 per cent over the next
decade, but this estimate was published in December, before the step-change in model capability we
saw early this year.
The IGR shows that productivity matters for the budget: productivity growth 0.4ppt higher than the
assumed 1.2% per year would reduce gross debt by 25ppts of GDP in 2065–66. Of course, this
assumes that fiscal policy does not respond to the faster trend growth that implies.

### Page 3

2. Australians will live longer
The IGR projects that life expectancy will continue to increase to 86.1 for males and 89.5 for females
by 2065, around five years longer than today. This presents both opportunities and challenges.
Older Australians are remaining in the workforce for longer, boosting labour supply and economic
activity. At the same time, rising demand for health, aged care and pension spending will place
increasing pressure on government budgets.

This is one area where AI, along with governance changes, could deliver outsized benefits. While an
ageing population will increase demand for government services, productivity-enhancing technologies
can improve service delivery and support prevention.
We know that the non-market sector (including health care, education and public administration) is the
largest it has ever been, with the expansion mainly driven by health services. Notwithstanding

measurement issues, we know that productivity levels in this sector has declined recently. Greater use
of evidence-based practice, preventive care, and AI-enabled technologies in health and aged care
could help improve productivity across this rapidly expanding part of the economy.

### Page 4

Demographic change will increase reliance on a smaller share of the population to fund the health,
aged care and other services required by a growing number of older Australians. We know that the
average fiscal contribution of an individual becomes negative later in life as government spending on
health and aged care rises. By contrast, people in their prime working years make the largest net
contribution to the budget through higher workforce participation and tax payments. 
This reinforces the importance of lifting productivity growth, both to support living standards and to help
meet rising spending pressures without placing an ever-greater burden on the working-age population.

3. NDIS reforms are crucial to budget sustainability, but knock on to state budgets 
The IGR highlights just how significant the NDIS reforms are for the fiscal outlook. Without the reforms,
payments would be around 1 percentage point of GDP higher over the medium term. The savings are
substantial and reinforce the importance of full implementation, a point we have previously made
(2026–27 Australian Federal Budget | Westpac IQ ). 
The report focuses on the Commonwealth budget, but the implications for state budgets could also be
significant. To the extent that some costs are shifted to states, a complete assessment of the reforms
requires a consolidated national fiscal perspective.

### Page 5

4. Bracket creep has its limits, and future spending will need to be funded elsewhere
Personal income tax receipts are projected to rise from 12.3% of GDP in 2025-26 to 14.1% of GDP by
2065-66, even with the Government capping tax as a share of the economy.
This highlights a structural challenge. As Australia ages, an increasing share of government revenue
will come from a relatively smaller working-age population, while the share of indirect taxes continues

to decline.
The IGR argues that relying ever more heavily on labour income taxation is unlikely to be a sustainable
long-run solution. Future governments may therefore need to look elsewhere for revenue growth. This
could include reforms to indirect taxes, particularly as fuel excise revenues come under pressure from
the transition to electric vehicles.
The report reinforces the need to modernise the tax base as activity shifts towards digital services and

intangible consumption. The revenue challenge is material, with the erosion of indirect tax bases
potentially amounting to close to 1ppt of GDP over coming decades, on top of the effective loss of
tobacco excise in recent years.
The terms of trade are assumed to remain broadly unchanged from their 2027–28 level, underpinning

### Page 6

company tax receipts. This bakes in the sharp near-term decline in iron ore prices that has been
assumed in every budget for more than a decade without eventuating. Despite this, the longer-run
trajectory is increasing uncertain as the world transitions towards net zero.
5. Higher costs of capital here to stay
We have long argued that the era of ultra-low interest rates is unlikely to return. The 2026 IGR
reinforces this view. Despite an improved fiscal position and lower projected debt than in the 2023 IGR,

interest payments are projected to be higher because government borrowing costs are assumed to
remain elevated relative to the post-GFC period.
Total interest payments are projected to rise from 0.9% of GDP in 2025–26 to 1.6% of GDP in 2032–33
before easing to 1.2% of GDP by 2065–66. Importantly, the primary cash balance (excluding interest
payments) is projected to remain in surplus until the mid-2050s. In other words, the budget is projected
to be in deficit largely because of the growing cost of servicing debt, rather than an underlying

imbalance between government revenues and expenditures.
The IGR’s sensitivity analysis highlights how important borrowing costs are for the fiscal outlook. If
bond yields average 5.4 per cent, broadly in line with current levels, the underlying cash deficit would
be around 0.5 percentage points of GDP larger and gross debt would be 6.6 percentage points of GDP
higher by 2065-66.

### Page 7

UPCOMING DATA
• UN General Assembly: President Trump will be meeting with seve -
ral foreign leaders including Ms. Takaichi and Messrs. Burnham, Xi,
and Zelensky.
• Advanced Economies: September flash PMIs (Wednesday/Thurs -
day).
• United States: Sept. Richmond manufacturing survey (Monday),
August durable goods orders (Friday).

• European Union: EC consumer confidence flash (Tuesday), Ger -
many September Ifo and France INSEE business climate (Thursday),
August credit and money supply (Friday).
ADVANCED ECONOMIES
EUROZONE / EUROPEAN UNION
ECB speakers temper hawkish tone even as incoming data points
to resilience. Several Governing Council members confirmed the
ECB might need to increase interest rates further but signaled they
saw no haste to do so (including President Lagarde and Vice-Pre -

sident Vujcic). (We foresee a final 25 bps hike in December).
In July, exports rose 1.2% m/m while imports fell 0.4% m/m; ex -
ports to the US rebounded (+6.0% m/m), offset by a similar decline
in sales to China. Industrial production fell less than expected in
July (-0.1% m/m, -0.3% m/m for manufacturing), held back by lower
motor vehicle output, while computer and electronic equipment
bounced back sharply. Forward-looking PMI and EC surveys signal

further improvement ahead. The ECB wage tracker points to a pick-
up in wage growth in 2027 after stabilising in H2 2026, with the
base-wage index reaching 2.9% y/y in June 2027—though not si -
gnaling “second-round effects” from the ongoing inflation spike.
Consumer inflation expectations (ECB survey) edged up in August,
however, with the median up 0.1 pp both for one year (3.0%) and
five-year (2.5%) ahead. The ECB’s Supervisory Banking Statistics re -

vealed a strong banking system as of end-Q2 2026. Capital ratios
remain at record highs (16% in Q2 2026 for CET1), whilst non-per -
forming loan (NPL) ratios were at record lows (2.2%). Return on
Equity reached a peak not seen since 2015 at 10.73, thanks to a
cost-to-income ratio at its lowest level since 2015 and stable net
interest margins.
Transatlantic relations: Two surprise rapprochements. On Friday,
President Trump announced the US had reached an agreement with

Denmark and Greenland giving the US “permanent control over
security and other needs” including by restricting the rights of non-
NATO members in the territory. Denmark confirmed the deal should
be signed soon. Also unexpectedly, President Von der Leyen used
her SOTEU speech to offer Canada “associate membership” in the
EU, an undefined status that does not exist in the EU treaties and
would require unanimous approval from the 27 member states.
France: Higher fiscal deficit and lower growth forecasts

overshadow positive news on business starts. Prime Minister S.
Lecornu announced that the budget deficit is expected to reach 5.4%
of GDP in 2026, versus 5% targeted and 5.1% in 2025. This overrun
stems mainly from interest payments, fuel subsidies, and below-
forecast growth. The draft budget for 2027 is expected to target
a deficit of 5% of GDP . Government spending would be completely
frozen (excluding interest payments and defence), and the fiscal

effort would aim to be more evenly distributed than in the past.
Meanwhile, the Banque de France (BdF) has lowered its growth
forecast from 0.5% to 0.4% for 2026 and left unchanged its forecast
for 2027 at 0.9%. The OAT-bund spread topped 100 bps on Friday,
its highest since 2012. More positively, business creations rose
16% y/y in August and 12% y/y YTD. The strongest growth was
seen in the information and communications sector (+57% y/y;
Ytd +46% y/y) and business services (+15% y/y; Ytd +19% y/y).

According to the BdF, business insolvencies increased by 4.6% y/y
after +5.1% y/y in June. They are declining in construction (-2.4%
y/y), stabilising in retail, but continuing to rise in certain service
sectors. According to Altarès, late payments fell to their lowest
level in three years. Wage growth accelerated to 2.4% y/y in Q2
from 2.1% y/y in Q1, mainly driven by the increase of the minimum
wage in June.
Germany: Confidence and producer price inflation rise to new

highs. The current economic situation index rose by 14 pts to its
highest level since May 2023 (at -47.1 in the ZEW survey for Sep -
tember. The Economic expectations index rose by 0.5 pts to 34.7,
its highest level since February 2026. Improvements were seen
across all sectors, with only construction and retail remaining be -
low their pre-Iran war levels. Wholesale prices rose by 6.8% y/y in
August (vs. +5.3% in July) due to energy prices, while PPI grew by

4.6% y/y (vs. +3.0% in July and +4.1% expected). Prices for interme -
diate goods rose by 6.1% (from 5.4%) due to a surge in metal prices.
UNITED KINGDOM
A hawkish BOE hold as inflation picks up and activity data reas -
sures. The Bank of England left the Bank Rate unchanged at 3,75%
(6-3 vote), as expected, but shifted its tone to suggest readiness
for a conditional 25bp 'insurance' hike to 4% in November. More
unexpectedly, the BOE announced significant quantitative tighte -

ning (QT) relief with a new Gilt divestment schedule through 2034
that features a pause in active sales until April 2027, direct sales
to the Treasury’s debt management office (instead of to the mar -
ket), and a cessation of sales for bonds maturing after 2049. Gilts
rallied on the news. Headline CPI accelerated to 3.1% y/y in August
(vs. 2.9% in July), meeting market expectations, primarily driven by
rising transport and fuel costs, notably diesel (+8.0% m/m). Core

CPI remained stable at 2.6% y/y. Retail sales volumes unexpecte -
dly rebounded to 0.5% m/m in August (2.4% y/y), despite a drop in
fuel sales. Unemployment held steady at 4.9% in July for the four -
th consecutive month (vs. 5.0% expected). Wage growth accelerated
slightly to 3.5% in the 3 months to July, driven by the public sector
(+6.7%).
UNITED STATES
Kevin Warsh’s Fed delivers. The Fed convincingly set aside doubts
regarding its seriousness about restoring price stability by not only

raising the Fed funds target range by 25bp to 3.75-4.00% (as widely
expected), but doing so unanimously, and with strong ownership
by Chair Warsh, who hawkishly described the move as “removing
a dose of accommodation”. 16 (out of 19) FOMC members now ex -
pect another hike by year-end, and 8 expect another to be neces -
sary thereafter. The FOMC statement and Summary of Economic
projections both expressed confidence in the strength of US eco -
nomy, with not a single member seeing risks tilted to the downside

(a first since 2017). The Committee foresees inflation returning to
its 2% target before 2029, with unemployment falling to 4.1% and
GDP growth revised higher and remaining above trend through

2028, i.e., immaculate disinflation. We expect at least two more rate
hikes, with the next one in December as our base case, but Octo -
ber not ruled out. In other news, in July, foreign private investors
turned net sellers of Treasury bonds and notes (USD -29bn) and US
equities (USD -10bn), while non-resident official investors turned

net buyers (USD +25bn and USD +14bn, respectively). However, pri -
vate investors’ cumulative net purchases of equities since the be -
ginning of the year exceed those of last year (+386 bn vs. +269 bn).
The cumulative net purchases of Treasuries by the official sector re -
main modest (+22 bn 7M26 vs. +10 bn 7M25). Latest data points
to continued strength in price pressures and activity, except in
housing: Further inflationary impulse is evident from import prices

(+7% y/y in August, a four-year high, largely driven by AI-related ca -
pital goods) and from the Philadelphia Fed index of delivery times
and backlog gauges hitting their highest levels since 2022. August
retail sales were robust (+1.2% m/m, +1.4% m/m for the core) and
jobless claims fell more than expected by 10k to 196k, one of the
lowest readings since 1969. Housing indicators weakened further in
August, with both building permits and housing starts falling (-2.7%

and 2.6% m/m, respectively). Separately, the Clarity Act failed in the
Senate before reaching the floor. The Act aimed to provide a federal
regulatory framework for cryptocurrencies and stablecoins and was
seen as a booster to their expansion.
JAPAN
An insufficiently hawkish rate hike as reflation continues. The
Bank of Japan (BOJ) raised its benchmark interest rate by 25 bps to
1.25%, a level not seen since 1995. The move had been expected,
and Governor Ueda tried to sound hawkish at the press conference,

stressing the focus had now shifted to preventing above-
target inflation. However, the lack of unanimity of the decision
disappointed markets (2 members appointed by Prime Minister
Takaichi dissented). The currency weakened markedly following the
decision, to 157 per dollar. Headline CPI was stable at 1.9% y/y in
August, as was core CPI excluding fresh food and fuel, holding at its
fastest pace in three months and pointing to relatively persistent

underlying demand-driven price pressures. As such, we expect the
BOJ to continue to tighten monetary policy to 1.75% by March 2027.
Exports rose 19.3% in August, topping market consensus, supported
by robust demand for AI-related chips. Industrial production fell
0.2% m/m in July, defying estimates of a 0.1% rise and marking
its first decline since March. Machinery orders remained strong,
increasing 11.2% y/y, despite a volatile m/m decline. The capacity

utilisation index rose 0.5% m/m to its highest level since December
2023. PM Takaichi has proposed a temporary food tax cut (from 8%
to 1%) through 2029. Despite government plans to avoid additional
borrowing, the reform faces market skepticism regarding long-term
fiscal discipline and internal party dissent.
EMERGING ECONOMIES
EUROPE, MIDDLE EAST, AFRICA
Czech Republic: Monetary policy on hold as expected at 3.75%.
The Central bank remains the most hawkish in the region and has

floated the possibility of a rate hike by year-end.
ASIA
China: A continued K-shaped growth. August activity data confirmed
the wide divergence between solid growth of external demand-
driven manufacturing sectors and sluggish growth of sectors
dependent on domestic demand. Industrial growth accelerated to
+5.2% y/y from +4.5% in July, notably supported by semiconductor
production (+20.6% y/y). The contraction in production of refined
products eased in August (-6.9% y/y vs. -16.8% in June-July),

following the recent rebound in Chinese crude oil imports.
Meanwhile, growth in services continued to slow (+4.1% y/y vs.
+4.3% in July). Household spending barely increased in August, as
suggested by weak retail sales (-0.4% y/y in volume), the continued
decline in bank loans to households (-1.6% y/y) and falling housing
sales (-16.2% y/y in volume). Total investment continued to contract
(-7.2% y/y in nominal terms in the first eight months of 2026).

Nevertheless, the Yuan hit a four-year high ahead of a Trump-Xi
meeting next week.
Rest of Asia: Inflation reacceleration in several economies. In India,
CPI inflation accelerated to 4.8% y/y in August from 4.5% in July,
with food inflation rising to 6% and core inflation at 4.2%. Inflation
is no longer confined to food and transport. With the US monetary
tightening, expectations of rise in policy rates by the end of the
year have increased, although the timing remains dependent on oil

prices and downward pressure on the rupee. In Malaysia, August
CPI at 1.9% y/y (vs. 1.8% in July) remains benign despite energy
price pressures. Elsewhere in SEA, inflation is mixed: Thailand at
2.5% (vs. 1.9% in July), Philippines almost stable at 6.1%, Indonesia
at 3.2% (vs. 2.9%) and Vietnam at 4.9% (vs. 4.5%).
LATIN AMERICA
Argentina: Second estimates of real GDP for Q2 confirm the
contraction (-0.6% q/q). All components of domestic demand fell,

especially private consumption (-2.4%). The contraction would have
been worse without the large positive contribution of net exports
(+1.6 pp). In y/y terms, growth decelerated to only 1.4%.
Brazil: The Selic rate was cut by 25 basis points to 13.75%. The
Central bank keeps a “meeting-by-meeting” prudential approach
to preserve its flexibility. Three weeks before the first round of
the presidential election (October 4), the Copom avoids giving any

strong signal that could be interpreted as a political stance.
ENERGY
Stabilisation at high levels: Brent oil prices remain above 100$/b
but stabilised at a slightly lower level than last week, on hopes of
a new diplomatic round in Iran and expectations of a rapid resto -
ration of the Saudi East-West pipeline. However, physical crude
markets remain severely dislocated (Brent physical/future spread is
above 20$/b against 3$/b on average in August) and diesel crack

spreads remain near their highs (above 90 $/b in Europe and
above 110 $/b in the US) as market tensions are stronger than
for crude. On Friday, President Macron called a special G7 Summit
to discuss additional release of Strategic Petroleum Reserves. Eu -
ropean gas prices were almost stable during the week (77 €/MWh
on Monday 21st). EU storage reached a better than expected 68.8%
of full capacity (September 17 th). In Asia, JKM continues to rise

(+7.5% w/w) on the back of unusually hot temperatures in Japan.

AGGREGATE ESTIMATES AND REVISIONS 
 26Q2 Y/Y earnings are expected to be 53.3%. Excluding the energy sector, the Y/Y earnings estimate is 49.5%. 
 Of the 492 companies in the S&P 500 that have reported earnings to date for 26Q2, 86.0% have reported earnings 
above analyst estimates. This compares to a long-term average of 67.5% and prior four quarter average of 80%. 
 26Q2 Y/Y revenue is expected to be 15.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the growth estimate is 13.5%. 
 77.3% of companies have reported 26Q2 revenue above analyst expectations. This compares to a long-term 
average of 62.8% and an average over the past four quarters of 77%. 
 For 26Q3, there have been 36 negative EPS preannouncements issued by S&P 500 corporations compared to 63 
positive EPS preannouncements. By dividing 36 by 63 the N/P ratio is 0.6 for the S&P 500 Index.  
 The forward four-quarter (26Q3– 27Q2) P/E ratio for the S&P 500 is 20.3. 
 During the week of Sep. 7, six S&P 500 companies are expected to report quarterly earnings.

There has been an increase in the share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 since the start of the quarter (to $858.9B 
from $687.1B). Two of the eleven sectors have experienced downward revisions to estimates.

Since May 1, the health care (-12.3%) and utilities (-1.5%) sectors have recorded the highest percentage decreases in 
earnings, while the communication services (100.1%) sector has recorded the highest percentage increase in 
earnings. Overall, share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 25% since the start of the quarter.

Since May 1, the health care (-9.3B) and utilities (-0.2B) sectors have recorded the highest dollar-level decreases in 
earnings, while the communication services (70B) sector has recorded the highest dollar-level increase in earnings. 
Overall, expected share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 171.7B since the start of the quarter.

The estimated earnings growth rate for the S&P 500 for 26Q2 is 53.3%. If the energy sector is excluded, the growth 
rate declines to 49.5%. The S&P 500 expects to see share-weighted earnings of $858.9B in 26Q2, compared to share-
weighted earnings of $560.2B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 503 constituents) in 25Q2. 
10 of the 11 sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in earnings relative to 25Q2. The Energy and 
Communication Services sectors have the highest earnings growth rates for the quarter, while the Health Care sector 
has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q2. 
The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (142.7%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $55.8B in 26Q2, 
compared to earnings of $23B in 25Q2. 4 of the 5 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher earnings 
than a year ago. The Oil & Gas Refining & Marketing (354.1%) and Integrated Oil & Gas (166.5%) sub-industries have 
the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate declines to 60.1%. 
The Communication Services sector has the second highest earnings growth rate (114.2%) of any sector. It is 
expected to earn $140B in 26Q2, compared to earnings of $65.3B in 25Q2. 7 of the 9 sub-industries in the sector are 
anticipated to see higher earnings than a year ago. The Cable & Satellite (397.4%) and Interactive Media & Services 
(174.3%) sub-industries have the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the 
growth rate declines to 3.5%. 
The Health Care sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (-6.5%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $65.9B in 
26Q2, compared to earnings of $70.5B in 25Q2. 2 of the 10 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower 
earnings than a year ago. The Biotechnology (-67.6%) and Pharmaceuticals (-9.2%) sub-industries have the lowest 
earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate improves to 22.7%.

STARMINE EARNINGS SURPRISE FORECAST 
Looking forward at quarterly performance, we use StarMine’s SmartEstimate® from LSEG to determine which 
companies in the S&P 500 are better poised to beat and miss earnings estimates. The SmartEstimate® is a weighted 
average of analyst estimates, with more weight given to more recent estimates and more accurate analysts. Our 
studies have shown that when the SmartEstimate® differs significantly from the consensus (I/B/E/S mean estimate), 
the Predicted Surprise accurately predicts the direction of earnings surprises or further revisions 70% of the time. 
When a significant Predicted Surprise for revenue is also present for the period, the accuracy improves to 78%.  
StarMine ARM from LSEG is an analyst revisions stock ranking model, designed to predict future changes in analyst 
sentiment. ARM incorporates more accurate earnings estimates through the SmartEstimate prediction service. ARM 
region rankings scores companies by region on a scale of 1 to 100 where 100 represents the most bullish sentiment.  
Over the next two weeks, six S&P 500 companies are expected to report earnings. Of these companies, 0 positive 
surprises and 0 negative surprises are expected from S&P 500 companies reporting quarterly results.

THE WEEKLY AGGREGATES REPORT
The Weekly Aggregates report provides updates on aggregate earnings revisions, growth rates and valuations. The 
numbers in the "Global Datapoints", "U.S. Datapoints" and "Ancillary Data" sections are updated weekly.
This report combines I/B/E/S's unrivaled historical earnings database, in depth coverage of Wall Street analysts' 
bottom-up corporate earnings estimates, and the analytic capabilities of the Proprietary Research group 
and desk-top solutions.

ITEM DEFINITIONS AND METHODOLOGY
3© 2026 LSEG.  All marks herein are used under licence. TRPR_82222_867
General:
All aggregates are bottom-up aggregates calculated from analysts' consensus forecasts unless otherwise noted. Bottom-up
aggregates are weighted calculations using the I/B/E/S data from LSEG share class monitored or the official index weight of
the index-provider. Top-down forecasts are compilations of strategists' estimates. I/B/E/S universe calculations are based on

all companies within a given market monitored by LSEG.
Benchmark index calculations, including those for official local market indices, the S&P 500 Index, and the FTSE World Equity
Indices are calculated using official index constituents and weightings. All index composites are representative of current
index constituents. Index constituents and weightings are updated monthly during options expiration (the Thursday before the
third Friday of every month) for every index except the S&P 500, the constituents of which are current as of the date of

calculation.
PEIGBY refers to the Price Earnings Inverse Government Bond Yield ratio, which measures equity valuation with respect to
the inverse-yield on long-term government bonds (now known as the 'Fed' model). All PEIGBY ratios are I/B/E/S universe-
specific except for the US ratio, where it is based on the S&P 500 Index.
Methodology:
For US and selected Canadian aggregates, calendar-year aggregation is performed using the sum of four quarters' estimates

and a quarterly pro-ration is applied to non-December year end companies' quarterly EPS estimates. Outside of the US and
Canada, all company-level data are calendarized prior to aggregation using the Compustat method of calendarization.
Aggregation is performed using the annual consensus EPS estimate and a May roll-back is applied to December year-end
markets. Aggregates for Japan, where most companies report in March, use an August rollback and a November rollback is

used for Australia and New Zealand, where the fiscal year-end for most companies is in June.
All 12-month-forward data for ex-US markets are calculated using a pro-rata weighting to annual EPS forecasts. The
weightings applied are a daily calibration out of 365 days relative to each constituent's individual fiscal year-end and the
current date.
All non-surprise data, including aggregate forecast earnings, forecast growth, and forward-looking valuation calculations are

weighted according to the I/B/E/S share class monitored for I/B/E/S universes or the weightings assigned to each constituent
in a given index. Aggregated earnings calculations are the equivalent of net profit aggregates, as they are derived from the
EPS forecasts aggregated to the net income level by the appropriate share weighting.
Earnings Aggregation:
For 12-month-forward earnings, the 12-month-forward EPS is used in place of the calendarized EPS in the formulas below.

I/B/E/S universe EPS Aggregate = Index EPS Aggregate =
Growth Calculations:
All growth calculations are the weighted year over year growth in earnings for the calendarized fiscal year period. The
formulas below use index shares for index growth calculations.
Calendarized Earnings Growth = 12-month-forward Growth =
Valuation:
All valuation calculations are the weighted-average price-earnings ratio for the fiscal period in question. The formulas below

use index shares for index P/E calculations.
Calendarized P/E Aggregate = 12-month-forward P/E =
Revisions Momentum:
Unless otherwise indicated, analysts' revisions momentum is a simple aggregation of the total number of estimates raised
divided by the total number of estimates lowered. Thus, any revisions ratio less than 1 indicates negative revisions
momentum while a ratio greater than 1 indicates positive revisions momentum, with 1 marking parity.

The Global Aggregates from LSEG product contains annual aggregates for universes included in this publication, including
supplemental aggregate data of over 100 items as well as history dating back to 1985 for North America and to 1987 for other
markets. To learn more about this product, contact your LSEG sales representative.
FTSE and the FTSE World Equity Indices are owned by FTSE International Limited.

### Page 2

Maximilian Uleer, Carolin Raab, Francesca Mazzali | Equity Outlook | September 2026
Europe is back and Germany’s potential
seems not yet fully appreciated by markets
In early 2025, we made a bold call: we argued that Europe’s 15-year history of underperformance has come to an end (MEGA and here). We
projected the P/E valuation gap between US and European equities to narrow from a record 9.0x at the end of 2024 to 4.3x. Since then, the
gap has indeed tightened to 4.7x. With Germany’s potential still being underestimated by markets, we expect more to come for Europe.

Looking ahead, we see several structural factors supporting Europe’s relative attractiveness:
– Valuations: After the tightening, we expect the gap to stabilize. Higher US growth justifies a premium but most of the below arguments
argue against a renewed widening.
– Earnings: We expect the European earnings recovery to continue, delivering double-digit growth in 2026. While US earnings are still
expected to grow faster, uncertainty around the sustainability of future growth, particularly in the Tech sector, remains elevated.

– Germany: The US and China are likely to continue growing faster than Europe, but much of that growth has been accompanied by
widening deficits and rising debt. US fiscal deficit and debt metrics now exceed European levels. At the same time, Germany’s fiscal pivot
could support a relative recovery in European growth, an opportunity that equity markets have yet to fully embrace. A recovery in global
manufacturing activity provides an additional boost to Europe’s economy. Germany could become the powertrain of Europe’s economic

recovery. Having reduced its debt-to-GDP ratio over the past decade despite macro headwinds, it now has the fiscal capacity to support
growth through a substantial spending plan including EUR 1tr in new debt. The economy is showing greater resilience than many expected,
manufacturing activity is improving, international investor interest is beginning to re-emerge and reforms are advancing. We estimate the
pension reform alone could unlock €10-15bn of annual inflows into European equities.

– Diversification: Europe’s equity market is well diversified across sectors, providing broader return drivers than more concentrated markets
such as the US and Emerging Markets. This helps cushion performance during sharp market downturns.
– AI: While Europe is unlikely to catch up with the leading AI developers, it is also less exposed to risks surrounding the monetisation of heavy
AI investment. Instead, it should benefit indirectly from AI spending through its advanced manufacturing capabilities, its exposure to AI

infrastructure and through growing AI adoption itself.
– Political stability: While Europe’s fragmented political landscape is a structural disadvantage, external pressures from US and Chinese
trade policies and ongoing geopolitical challenges have strengthened coordination across the EU on trade, defence and fiscal matters.

### Page 13

Deep dive: Germany

### Page 14

Maximilian Uleer, Carolin Raab, Francesca Mazzali | Equity Outlook | September 2026
Germany has the potential to become the driving force behind Europe’s economic revival. Despite years of macro headwinds, it
reduced its debt ratio over the past decade, creating the fiscal space for a major spending package. Economic activity is
stabilising, manufacturing conditions are improving, reforms are progressing, and global investor interest is returning. Germany

has repeatedly demonstrated an ability to adapt and reinvent itself, with major reform waves following the coalition collapses
of 1966 and 1982 and the implementation of Agenda 2010 in the early 2000s.
– Fiscal: Few countries have faced as many challenges as Germany in recent years: rising Chinese competition in key industries,
the energy shock following Russia’s invasion of Ukraine, large-scale immigration, US tariffs, and a three-year manufacturing

downturn. Despite these headwinds, Germany is the only G7 economy to have reduced its debt-to-GDP ratio over the past
decade. The government plans to raise around €1tn of additional debt by 2030, on top of the current debt stock of €2.8tn. Net
credit borrowing is expected to increase from €180bn in 2026 to more than €200bn in 2027, while spending on defence and
investment is set to reach roughly €120bn each this year, up more than 30% year-on-year.

– Macro: Germany's economy is showing greater resilience towards the ongoing geopolitical headwinds than expected. The
manufacturing PMI climbed above 54 in August, its strongest reading in over four years, and both the Ifo and ZEW surveys
surprised positively. Factory orders are gradually improving and GDP growth in H1 exceeded expectations, prompting
consensus GDP forecasts to be revised up. Although higher energy prices may create short-term headwinds, the burden

remains manageable, with energy costs representing just slightly more than 1% of sales for listed German companies.
– Politics: Germany's reform momentum is strengthening. The government is combining fiscal stimulus with measures to lower
energy costs, boost corporate investment, accelerate infrastructure projects, reform the labour market, and modernise the
pension system. We estimate the pension reform alone could unlock €60-100bn of annual capital market inflows. At the same

time, political risks appear manageable. Regional elections are unlikely to affect federal policymaking, and we see little risk to
the government's reform agenda. If anything, weaker results for the coalition parties could increase pressure to accelerate
reforms.
– Equities: Markets have yet to price in Germany's improving outlook. While economic indicators continue to strengthen, investor
flows into German equities remain weak and the MDAX has lagged the DAX by 7% since late June. As the index with the

greatest exposure to the domestic economy, the MDAX is the purest listed play on Germany's recovery and has historically
shown a much stronger relationship with German GDP growth. A combination of fiscal stimulus, structural reforms, and more
industry-friendly policies creates substantial catch-up potential following years of underperformance. Importantly, valuations
remain attractive, with the MDAX trading at its cheapest relative level in history.

Don’t underestimate Germany’s
capacity to change

– The German government plans to increase debt by more

Regional elections
– Contrary to many concerns, the Saxony-Anhalt election

– Regional elections have limited implications for federal

derailing the government's reform agenda. If anything,
weaker results for coalition parties could increase pressure to

accelerate reforms.

### Page 33

Maximilian Uleer, Carolin Raab, Francesca Mazzali | Equity Outlook | September 2026
Key takeaways
Germany: pension reform
– The planned reform of Germany’s pension system could
generate significant inflows into European equities by
shifting retirement savings towards capital markets.
– On the private pension side, the new “old-age provision
account” encourages savers to move funds from low-yield
deposits and insurance products into higher-return
investments. If half of the eligible population invests the

annual amount required to receive the full tax benefit
(€2,300), inflows could reach €50bn per year.
– We also expect assets to migrate from the existing “Riester”
system. With total estimated Riester assets of €150bn, a 10%
annual reallocation would generate a further €15bn of
inflows.
– In parallel, the government plans to introduce a funded
component to the statutory pension system, equivalent to 2
pp of gross salaries, to be invested through a central public

pension fund. Contributions are planned to rise from 0.5pp in
2028 to 2pp by 2031, implying annual inflows of €8bn
initially and €30-35bn by 2031, according to our economists.
– Overall, we estimate these reforms could generate €60-
100bn of additional annual capital market inflows, of which
around €10-15bn (15%) may find its way into European
equities. To put this into context: the top 50 largest
European ETFs saw inflows of c. €40bn in 2025.

Key takeaways
German reforms: example from the past
– In 2023, Germany introduced the Onshore Wind
Act and revised the Offshore Wind Energy Act to
accelerate the energy transition and streamline
renewable energy development.
– For the first time, federal states face legally
binding requirements to allocate land for wind
power. The share of Germany's land designated for

onshore wind is set to increase to 1.4% by 2027
and 2.0% by 2032.
– Policy reforms are already delivering results. In H1
2025, Germany approved 20.8 GW of new onshore
wind capacity, nearly four times the level seen in
2022, marking a record pace of expansion.

### Page 40

Maximilian Uleer, Carolin Raab, Francesca Mazzali | Equity Outlook | September 2026
Key takeaways
German reforms: example from the past
– Facing stagnant growth and rising unemployment, Germany
launched a series of labour-market reforms between 2002
and 2005, beginning with the Hartz Commission and
culminating in Agenda 2010.
– The reform package tightened unemployment benefits,
expanded labour-market flexibility, improved job placement,
and reduced income and corporate taxes to strengthen

incentives for work and investment.
– While initially controversial, the reforms helped restore
confidence: GDP growth turned positive in 2004, consumer
sentiment improved, and equity markets rebounded.

Document 321: Sea change_ the market implications of the threat to the maritime commons (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Sea change_ the market implications of the threat to the maritime commons (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 18
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
Authors
Oliver Harvey
Macro Strategist
+44(20)754-51947
Deutsche Bank
Research Institute
Sea change: the market
implications of the threat
to the maritime commons
September 25, 2026
The sea is overwhelmingly the most important conduit for global commerce. It is
used to transport 80% of the world's trade in goods and 90% of the trade in oil.
Even in a world in which services trade is likely to grow in importance owing to AI,
the world's oceans remain supreme, with submarine fibre optic cables carrying

close to all of the world's internet traffic.
Painstaking multilateral negotiations after 1945 established a legal architecture
to enable the almost frictionless movement of commerce across the seas and
delimit nations rights' to restrict freedom of navigation. This process learned
from the hugely damaging experience of two world wars, and preceding centuries
of maritime warfare which had devastating effect on international trade and
finance.
Recent years have seen a growing threat to this paradigm. The frequency of

maritime supply shocks is increasing at an alarming rate. After the COVID
pandemic generated significant friction in maritime supply chains, geopolitics
has come to the fore, including Russia's Black Sea blockade in 2022, Houthi
attacks on commercial shipping in the Red Sea in 2023, a resurgence in piracy off
the Horn of Africa and most recently the twin blockades of the Strait of Hormuz
and Bab el Mandeb Strait. This has dovetailed with an apparently reduced US

willingness to uphold freedom of navigation, perhaps as part of a broader trend
away from supporting the 'rules of the game' that underpin an open international
economy. In short, the sea is increasingly a theatre of geoeconomic competition,
a trend that could accelerate further with climate induced changes to Arctic
maritime routes.
We argue that economists and market participants should be more alive to these
risks. The lexicon of tariffs has dominated the market's attention over recent

years, but tariff induced supply shocks have proved far less damaging than
physical supply shocks. Disruption to ocean commerce has generated outsized
impacts on both the prices of goods and the volumes of cross border trade.
We distinguish between three forms of maritime shocks of increasing intensity.
From localised disruption that can generate short lived effects on prices, to more
serious regional disruptions like the one at present in the Gulf, to global events

### Page 2

Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
Introduction
It is difficult to overstate the importance of the sea when it comes to world trade.
Maritime transport moves four fifths of all goods traded world wide.1 Over 90% of
exported oil is seabourne.2After energy, coal, iron ore, grain and manufactured
goods make up the remaining 60% of all seabourne cargo. The 'container
revolution,' starting from the late 1960s, dramatically improved the handling
efficiency of cargo, and has been seen as one of the primary drivers of global trade

growth during the second half of the 20th century. 3The sea doesn't just ship goods,
its floor carries electricity and data, with fiber optic submarine cables carrying 99%
of the world's international internet traffic.4
If the world's oceans were the arteries for the world's second great globalisation
from 1945 onwards, the third decade of the 21st century have seen their disruption.
The last six years have seen a dramatic rise in maritime based supply shocks.

The 2020 pandemic challenged supply chains globally. This included container
shipping, which faced public health restrictions causing a large share of the global
carrier fleet to be held in port. More recently, geopolitical events have come to the
fore. Russia's 2022 invasion of Ukraine was accompanied by a maritime exclusion
zone in the Sea of Azov and parts of the Black Sea, as well as illegal Russian attacks
on merchant shipping, paralysing trade in the region. In response to Israel's military

activities in Gaza following the 7/10 attacks, the Houthis began illegal attacks on
commercial shipping transiting the Red Sea, which resulted in a severe fall in trade
volumes. After an international task force formed in 2009 successfully curbed
Somali pirate attacks around the Horn of Africa, recent years have seen a
resurgence of hijacking. And this year has seen the twin shocks of Iran's closure of
the strait of Hormuz and the Houthi blockade on Saudi shipping.

As a result, risk based analysis of maritime choke points has proliferated. We argue,
however, that disruptions of this kind should be seen not as isolated risk events, but
as part of a trend. An understanding of the legal and historical basis of maritime
commerce suggests that the rules of the game are changing. Near frictionless
maritime passage is being disrupted by simultaneous challenges to its legal
architecture and, more crucially, the US and its Western partners' willingness to

enforce these rules. The Trump administration's recent toying with tariffs on the
strait of Hormuz, a break from established international norms, is but one example.
There has been far more oxygen dedicated by markets to discussing the impact of
tariffs in recent years by the economic community, but the impact of tariffs on trade
are relatively trivial compared to the potential disruption caused by maritime trade
blockages. Indeed, in this piece, we argue that physical supply shocks to trade can

be among the most disruptive types of economic event. The global shipping
industry is highly sophisticated, but its network can also prove fragile. We identify
three forms of supply shock of increasing intensity: shocks that increase freight
rates, regional blockades with some scope for rerouting or substitution of maritime
routes, and global disruptions to the freedom of the seas.
1 UNCTAD statistical bulletin, April 2025
2 Stanford Energy, March 2025

3 Bernhofen et al, "Estimating the effects of the container revolution on world trade." Journal of
international Economics, January 2016
4 United Nations
25 September 2026
FX Special Report

### Page 3

FX Special Report
Our work over the last eighteen months has built a framework to understand the
implications of shifts in US political economy for the international economy. In
particular, we have examined how the US's increasing unwillingness to act as a
'benevolent hegemon' upholding the international order could affect markets,

focusing on the dollar, global imbalances and interest rates, and the more frequent
use of economic coercion.5 Shifts in the maritime arena are yet another example of
this shift. Increasingly, the sea is becoming a theatre of geoeconomic competition,
while the 'Pax Americana' that has thus far mostly guaranteed free movement
across the seas is coming under threat. We end the the report with specific case
studies on the potential implications of a disruption to commerce around critical

maritime routes.
A short history of maritime law and war
"The bare principles of international maritime law are no more than a reasonable
compromise between a belligerent's contention, that he should be permitted to stop
an enemy's commerce, wherever it is to be found, and the neutral's rejoinder, that he
is no-one's enemy, and he has a right to trade and traffic with any country with which
he is at peace." "Official Foreign Office Account of Britain's Economic Blockade

of Germany, 1914-1919," published 1937
The maritime blockade dates back to the Peloponnesian Wars in 405 BC, but it was
not until the early 19th century that a recognisably modern framework of the rights
of belligerents and neutrals, and legal basis around maritime commerce, started to
be drawn up. The blockade and counter-blockade  of Napoleonic France saw
precedents established for what was acceptable for 'prizes,' namely the ships and
goods that warring parties were allowed to seize and confiscate, the  law of

'blockade,' and other important concepts.

### Page 4

Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
6 Leah Cole, Lawfare, Not Warfare, Harvard Kennedy School report
25 September 2026
FX Special Report
These were developed further during the mid-19th century as the rapid rise in global
trade led merchants, manufacturers  and governments  to push for  greater
protections for mercantile shipping. The 1856 Declaration of Paris provided more
stringent legal definitions of blockade and what constituted the contraband of war
(trade between neutrals and belligerents that assisted the war effort and could

therefore legitimately be seized). This was developed upon by the 1907 Hague
Conference. Britain, then the preeminent economic and maritime power of the day,
took the lead at these conferences, but also pursued a strategy of Imperial Defence
in the late 19th century based on the fortification of its coaling stations and ports
across the globe, as well as developing a 'two-power' naval standard,' whereby its
naval strength would be at least as powerful as its next two rivals combined.

This underscores that while the establishment of legal norms helped maritime
commerce become more predicable and orderly, ultimately their survival depended
on the willingness of their signatories to enforce them. Maritime economic warfare
during the 19th and 20th centuries often saw these norms suspended. The results
could be devastating.  Britain's economic blockade of Germany  during WW1
debilitated the German war effort as well as causing mass starvation. It saw a ~65%

collapse in German exports based on trade data from the Federico Tena database
(figure 1). The Union's blockade of Confederate Ports during the American Civil War
crippled the confederacy's war effort, with export volumes to Britain falling by close
to 100%.
After the disruption caused by the second world war, the growth in global trade over
the second half of the 20th century once again motivated the codification of rules of
the sea. Successive UN conferences from the mid 1950s onwards culminated in the

1982 UN Convention on the Law of the Sea (UNCLOS),  which settled several
ambiguities in international  law. Among these  were geographically defined
territorial rights for maritime states, including the twelve mile territorial limit, and
the concept of innocent passage, as well as the status of international straits and
transit passage. Another increasingly important provision of UNCLOS was the
establishment of a legal framework  governing the laying and protection of

undersea cables.
Although the Convention was ratified by almost every industrial nation, the United
States has remained a notable exception. Initially, this was due to concerns in the
Reagan presidency that to do so would transfer US claims on seabed ownership.
Since then, the US has come close to ratification  under Presidents Bush and
Obama. Nevertheless, as we shall see below, up until recently the US has largely
upheld and enforced the broad provisions of UNCLOS through its Navy and Coast

Guard as accepted customary law, with the exception of Part XI of the agreement
which regulates the mineral resources of the seabed. Indeed, the US has been
described as the 'Convention's best supporter,' despite not ratifying the treaty. 6
Some important principles in present day maritime law
Territorial waters. The sovereign territory of coastal states. Under the 1982 UN
Convention on the Law of the Sea this was defined as up to 12 nautical miles from

the low water mark of a state's coast. Some ambiguities  exist with respect to
defining this limit, including whether states utilise continuous baselines to define
their coast, or straight baselines (mapping coastal points from a to b in a straight

### Page 5

Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
Freedom of the seas and the Pax Americana
Despite certain flaws, UNCLOS is widely regarded by practitioners, governments
and scholars as one of the most successful multilateral treaties ever created.
Among its achievements have been the regulation of international maritime trade,
the de-limitation of coastal territorial and economic claims and ultimately the
preservation of the right to free navigation on the high seas.
The United States, along with other Western partners, have been active enforcers

of UNCLOS. In the late 1980s, the Reagan administration launched Operation
Earnest Will to protect commercial shipping from mines and missiles fired by Iran
in the Gulf of Hormuz. 11 More recently, the United States was the largest
contributor to the multinational anti piracy Operation Ocean Shield in the Horn of
Africa between 2009-2016. Outside of military operations, one of the main ways
through which the United States has freedom of the seas is through Freedom of

Navigation Operations (FONOPS), which involve the transiting of naval vessels
through certain stretches of water such as the Taiwan strait in order to push back
7 More details on legal definition.
8 More details on legal definition.
9 More details on legal definition
10 See Naval College Review of Theories of Blockade, Winter, 2021
11 Revisiting the Tanker War, WOTR, March 2024
25 September 2026
FX Special Report
line). 7
International strait. A passage of water used for international navigation between

one part of the high seas or exclusive economic zone and another. Where the strait
is narrow enough such that it is entirely made of up of certain states' territorial
waters, the law of transit passage applies. This law provides freedom of movement
for ships and aircraft, provided their journey is for the purpose of continuous and
expeditious transit. The concept of transit passage was recognized in the 1982 UN
Convention in the Law of the Sea.8
Innocent passage. The concept of innocent passage is more restrictive than transit

passage. It provides a right of passage through the territorial sea of all states, "‘so
long as it is not prejudicial to the peace, good order, or security of the coastal State."
Innocent passage can be suspended or safe routes established if there are risks to
pollution, smuggling, security concerns etc. The regime of innocent passage was
recognised in the 1958 Convention on the Territorial Sea. 9
Blockade. A blockade constitutes a maritime or airborne operation to prevent

enemy and neutrals from entering an airport, port or coastal area. There  is no
established body of international law that defines a military blockade, but military
custom generally agrees on several principles. 10 A blockade must declared and
notified to all states, including details such as geographical scope. A blockade
needs to be 'effective,' in other words appropriately policed by military or other
means. Finally, it must be impartial, and not applied on an ad hoc or discriminatory

basis to neutral vessels of different states. A 'maritime exclusion zone' or 'maritime
interdiction zone' constitute less severe versions of blockades. In the former case,
they are announced by states as part of military operations whereby enemy ships
in the area are liable to be fired upon. In the latter case, they are usually introduced
as part of counter-terrorism, piracy or smuggling operations, whereby ships are
liable to be boarded and searched as part of operations.

against territorial claims or other navigational restrictions seen as out of line with
international law.12

Prior to the Iran war, the most visible challenge to freedom of navigation in the
Middle East region was the start of Houthi attacks on commercial shipping in the
Red Sea. Although initially focused on ships with unspecified 'links' to Israel, these

attacks broadened out to incorporate a wide range of ships, dramatically reducing
traffic flows through Suez since 2023. A useful visual manifestation of this are
Egyptian Suez revenue numbers which have still to meaningfully recover from the
drop of traffic (figure 3). Operation Prosperity Guardian, the taskforce launched by
the United States was largely ineffective in restoring commercial traffic. This
12 for an example of FONOPS, see US Navy website. The US, and other international navies, also enforce

operations

25 September 2026
FX Special Report
blockade was subsequently expanded by the Houthis in July this year to
incorporate attacks on Saudi vessels and energy infrastructure.
Another important development has been the growing assertiveness of actors in
the South China Sea. Data from the Center for Strategic and International Studies

suggests that occurrences of maritime assertiveness have dramatically increased
over the last decade (figure 4). The CSIS notes that even if incidents have moved
away from the outright use of force towards more coercive and demonstrative
actions, China, the Philippines and Vietnam have each sought to both claim and
counter each other's  military/security, fisheries and natural resource rights. 13
Tension has been especially pronounced in the Taiwan strait, with China objecting

to FONOPs from the US, UK, New Zealand, Australia and Germany in recent years,
while both Taiwan and China have conducted military exercises in the strait.
Still further threats to maritime security have come from non-state actors or as a
result of asymmetric activities designed to undermine or challenge commercial
networks. One of these has been the resurgence of pirate attacks off the Horn of
Africa, with 15 reported incidents so far this year after joint-coalition operations had

all but quashed Somali pirate attacks over a decade ago (figure 5). 14 Another
extremely important area of maritime connectivity that is seemingly under threat
concerns undersea cables and pipelines. After incidents such as the sabotage of the
Nord Stream 2 pipeline in 2022 and more recently telecommunications cables such
as the C-Lion in the Baltic (figure 6), there is increasing focus among policymakers
as to the vulnerability and economic cost of attacks on sub-sea infrastructure.

Why is Hormuz so important? The blockade of the straits of Hormuz began in early
March this year, shortly after the instigation of US and Israeli military attacks on
Iran. Iran's blockade, the US counter-blockade and the subsequent MoU adopted
in June this  year (in practice suspended later that  month),  represent major
departures from the rules based maritime framework enforced by the US and other
Western allies.
First, by deliberately targeting neutral vessels, Iran's 'blockade' was  a clear

violation of both UNCLOS and the established military practice of blockade. This is
simply the latest of numerous examples of Iran's flouting of international law and
transit rights through the strait, albeit probably the most severe recent example.
Second, while the US's initial blockade on Iranian ships headed to or departing from
Iranian ports had a solid legals basis, the subsequent expansion of this blockade at
a global level, including ships of any flag state deemed to be carrying contraband

of war, has been widely considered to be legal overreach.15
Third, Iran's plans to impose a transit toll on vessels through the strait of up to 7%
of cargo value is a violation of transit rights established under UNCLOS. Iran is not
a signatory to the convention, and it has argued that its actions are permissible
under the narrower definition of the rights of innocent passage. Nevertheless, even
under the narrower 1958 Convention on the Territorial Sea to which Iran is a

signatory, this action is considered by maritime law experts to be illegal.16

### Page 8

Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
From global commons to economic warfare?
Markets should care about these developments for the simple reason that supply
shocks emanating from major disruptions to maritime trade and digital routes can
have severe global economic ramifications. In previous research we have
discussed how shifts in US policy over recent years have called into question the
maintenance of 'global public goods,' such an open international economic

environment. Much in the same way as the Trump administration's increased use
of economic coercion using tariffs has undermined confidence in multilateral trade
institutions and the 'rules of the game' when it comes to settling trade disputes,
should the US cease to enforce international maritime law, the risk could be that a
'race to the bottom' ensues in which state actors can leverage maritime coercion for
political or economic purposes.
17 FT Alphaville's coverage of this issue in an August 3 rd report appears to disregard widely accepted

international customary maritime law as well as UNCLOS
18 See, Lowy Institute Report, 24th October 2025

20 Greenland will be strategically important for both of these routes, which may have motivated US

FX Special Report
Most surprising, however, has been President Trump's apparent endorsement of a
toll based system, to apparently  enable the US to recoup the  cost of military

operations on Iran. Although some analysts have pointed to navigational or tug fees
as offering a potential precedent for these actions, this is misleading. Navigational
assistance or tugs are supplied for the safe passage of commercial vessels (e.g. in
busy shipping lanes with physical obstacles) and consistent with UNCLOS.17 By
contrast, the US's willingness to countenance transit tolls  as an acceptable
resolution mechanism to the crisis in turn calls into question the White House's

support for the post 1945 architecture. This commitment has also been challenged
by other actions taken by the Trump administration, including the recent push for
deep sea mining on the US continental shelf, in violation of UNCLOS's provisions
for the deep sea bed being a common global good.18 Still a further concern with
respect to the US's posture towards maritime security has been the  dramatic
reduction in US Navy FONOPS in the strait of Taiwan, which have fallen more than

50% from the previous Biden and Trump administrations.19
Looking ahead, it seems increasingly likely that the sea becomes another theatre of
geoeconomic competition, which may in turn be motivating US policy actions.
When it comes to Greenland and the Arctic, for example, climate induced changes
to maritime routes, especially the possible emergence of viable routes through the
Northwest Passage and Transpolar Sea Route, could have informed recent US

policy shifts.20 Similarly, there have been reports that  Russia is considering
withdrawing from UNCLOS on account of territorial claims over  the Arctic.
UNCLOS governs the Arctic, although a number of states have recently sought to
extend the limit of their claims on the continental shelf in that region.21
Further examples of increased US attention on strategic maritime considerations in
its hemisphere were the respective export blockade on Venezuela earlier this year,

whose legality was a subject of discussion  although ultimately fell under the
umbrella of sanctions enforcement, fuel blockade in Cuba and attention directed
towards the Panama Canal.

Surprisingly, maritime trade disruptions has still meaningfully lagged behind tariffs
in the market lexicon (see Bloomberg news trend for 'tariffs' and 'blockade' figure
7). Recent years has shown that physical supply shocks have larger economic

effects than tariffs, however. It is difficult to detect a protracted impact on US import
prices, for example, from US tariffs imposed either in the first or second Trump
administration, but the impact from the pandemic, Russian invasion of Ukraine and
more recently the Hormuz crisis has been much more pronounced (figure 8). More
formally, the IMF recently modelled the impact of port delays on US inflation at port
and product level, finding that a one hundred hour delay in ports lifts US inflation by

50bp over the following five months.22
The economic impact extends well beyond price effects. For countries that are
especially exposed to maritime imports, with limited substitutability in the near
term for affected products, import volumes will also be hit. This was evident during
the recent disruption to energy markets in Asia, which saw Bangladesh, South
Korea, Thailand and Vietnam imposing energy rationing, and work from home
requirements ordered in Indonesia and Malaysia. Figures 9 and 10 track import

volumes in Korea and China and clearly demonstrate the impact both on energy
imports (first chart) and overall import volumes (second chart) of recent maritime
disruptions. In Pakistan, the force majeure declared by Qatar on LNG deliveries has
forced Pakistan to instigate widespread load shedding on its electrical grid.
At a global level, the IMF's downgrades to global growth on account of tariffs last
year amounted to 0.2% over two years, but its downgrade in the aftermath of the

Ukraine war was a cumulative 1%.23 In short, then, in a world in which supply
shocks have become the dominant concern for economic policymakers, the risks
to maritime commerce should occupy the first rank of worry.

Assessing the importance of maritime shocks
With this in mind, the first step for market participants to be better prepared for
maritime shocks is to distinguish between their severity. We distinguish between
three broad kinds of shocks, and identify some salient characteristics of each.
1.Localised maritime disruption, factors generating higher insurance
costs, country specific sanctions.

Examples of the least severe, but still relevant, maritime supply shock would
include the sanctioning of Russian seaborne oil by the United States and EU after
2022, Houthi attacks on commercial shipping in the Red Sea in 2023 or pirate
attacks off the Horn of Africa.
More limited maritime shocks of this nature can be disruptive, but their economic
effects are generally short lived owing to the capacity of the international shipping
industry to substitute routes or exporters or otherwise manage risks. They are more

likely to manifest themselves in terms of price rather than volume effects.
Important transmission mechanisms for prices are increased freight rates, which in
turn owe to higher insurance costs or demand bottlenecks. This was evident, for
example, during the surge in Somali piracy attacks in 2011 which resulted in
increased costs for the shipping industry due to ransom and kidnap insurance, but
in many cases did not stop ships transiting close to the horn of Africa.24 In these

more localised disruptions, shipping companies can choose to re-route. This was
the case following the Houthi attacks in 2023 which saw numerous vessels choose
to take the lengthier Cape of Good Hope route from Europe to Asia and is reflected
in the recent rise in ship ton miles relative to ship tons transported internationally
according to UN data (figure 12). Similarly, in response to Russia's blockade of the
Black Sea, Ukraine was able to successively reroute a proportion of its grain exports

via the Danube or overland routes.25 Still a third option available to ships in these
more limited disruptions include re-flagging vessels. Russia has employed re-
flagging of oil carriers as part of its 'shadow fleet' following Western sanctions.

Another example of more limited maritime disruption would be to undersea cables.
A recent UK House of Lords Joint Committee addresses the risks associated with
the UK's extensive network of over 60 undersea cables. It concluded that the
severing of a number of cables was manageable from a communication continuity
perspective due to redundancy and rerouting, but that a severe disruption (large
